Here, we present EgoBrain -- the world's first large-scale, temporally aligned multimodal dataset that synchronizes first-person (egocentric) vision and EEG of human brain over extended periods of time, establishing a new paradigm for human-centered behavior analysis. This dataset comprises 61 hours of synchronized 32-channel EEG recordings and first-person video from 40 participants engaged in 29 categories of daily activities. We then developed a multimodal learning framework to fuse EEG and vision for action understanding, validated across both cross-subject and cross-environment challenges, achieving an action recognition accuracy of 66.70%. EgoBrain paves the way toward a unified framework for multimodal and egocentric brain-computer interfaces, bridging neural signals and first-person perception. Our dataset and code are publicly available at: https://huggingface.co/datasets/ut-vision/EgoBrain and https://github.com/ut-vision/EgoBrain .</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ICLR 2026. | **[How Much Reconstruction Does Quantum Machine Learning Need? Late Fusion of Independently Trained Quantum Subcircuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05595v1)** | 2026-08-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Circuit cutting lets a large quantum neural network (QNN) run as independent subcircuits on small devices, but rebuilding its outputs by reconstruction carries a classical sampling overhead exponential in the number of cuts - the dominant runtime cost in prior work. We ask whether, for machine-learning tasks, this step is necessary, and replace it with late fusion: each subcircuit is trained and measured independently, and a small classical head combines their outputs - a linear-cost, decision-level combination borrowed from multimodal learning. To characterize the trade-off we introduce a quantumness dial $Q$, a tunable reconstruction budget interpolating from pure fusion to full reconstruction, and a cut-entanglement diagnostic that indicates how much reconstruction a task needs (Spearman $ρ=0.59$ over $104$ runs). Across synthetic and standard datasets, independently trained late fusion matches full reconstruction accuracy within $0.04$ at every point of the controlled sweep and on every classical benchmark, at exponentially lower cost; it is also markedly more robust to shot and device noise. Controlled entangled-data experiments locate the boundary where fusion must fail. We do not claim advantage over classical machine learning - consistent with recent benchmarking, quantum offers no accuracy edge on these datasets. Late fusion is thus an efficient, noise-robust, self-characterizing alternative to reconstruction for circuit-cutting QML.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 6 figures. Includes technical appendix</p></details> |

Using clinical information as an anchor, we then introduce a dual-level interaction mechanism: 1) a local prototype association (LPA) module based on cross-attention to explicitly learn token-level correspondences between different modalities, and 2) a global feature alignment (GFA) loss based on Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) to implicitly enhance cross-modal distribution consistency. In this paper, we first analyze the impact of cross-modal fusion bias in MAD via the Fisher Information Matrix. Then, grounded in these findings, we propose UCFB, a simple yet effective plug-and-play framework designed to mitigate cross-modal fusion bias in MAD. It achieves this by jointly employing Fisher-information-guided dynamic calibration to adjust modality-specific regularization weights and canonical similarity analysis to improve inter-modal interactions. | **[SAGE: An Expert-Annotated South Asian GI Endoscopy Dataset for Multimodal Learning and Hallucination Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22144v2)** | 2026-08-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Gastrointestinal cancers represent a growing health burden in the South Asian region, driven largely by rapid changes in socio-economic conditions and lifestyle habits. Yet, almost all open-source, publicly available datasets are predominantly collected from the European region, with minimal representation from the South Asian region. The lack of open-source GI datasets from diverse geographic regions has made it difficult to assess whether population bias is present in existing models, and to develop geographically inclusive AI tools for automated GI diagnosis. To address this gap, we introduce SAGE: An Expert-Annotated South Asian GI Endoscopy dataset for Multimodal Learning and Hallucination Analysis, for image captioning, multi-label classification, and VQA tasks. It consists of 1,300 images, their captions along with hallucination tag, 18 labels and 14,276 question-answer pairs making it well-suited for diverse range of tasks including classification, benchmarking, and fine-tuning LMMs. We further conducted benchmarking of task-specific models, like multi-class classifiers on the effect of population shift which reveals that such models suffers the most with an average F1 score drop of 54 points on South Asian dataset. | **[JoVA: Unified Multimodal Learning for Joint Video-Audio Generation and Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.13677v2)** | 2026-07-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we present JoVA, a streamlined framework that unifies joint video-audio generation and editing. While existing methods often rely on fragmented, task-specific architectures or complex fusion mechanisms, JoVA employs native joint representation learning for direct video, audio, and text interaction in a dual-branch architecture. This design eliminates redundant alignment modules and effectively unifies diverse multimodal tasks within a single model. Project page: https://visual-ai.github.io/jova</p></details> | ECCV 2026 |
| **[SPICE: Synergy and Partial Information Based Curriculum Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16639v2)** | 2026-07-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal learning exploits complementary information across heterogeneous modalities. The informativeness of each modality can vary widely across samples and training stages. Existing multimodal curriculum learning strategies often assume that the relative complexity of samples remains unchanged throughout training and therefore cannot adapt to model evolution. Guided by Partial Information Decomposition (PID) theory, our approach decomposes multimodal interactions into redundant, unique, and synergistic information components, enabling an interpretable and dynamic characterization of sample complexity. Building on this decomposition, we design a progressive curriculum that evolves throughout training, allowing the model to transition from learning shared cross-modal cues to modality-specific patterns and, finally, to complex synergistic interactions. Adapting to model evolution, sample ordering is refined in real-time using PID information estimates derived from unimodal and multimodal predictions. Experiments across multiple multimodal benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over conventional training and state-of-the-art baselines, highlighting the effectiveness of PID information decomposition and adaptive sample ordering for multimodal curriculum learning.</p></details> |  |

## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[SpecMRL: Redesign Matryoshka Representation Learning within Parametric Spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26824v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) is designed to support flexible representation sizes by jointly optimizing nested prefixes. While effective, this design lacks an explicit constraint to ensure that the leading portion provides a suitable compact encoding. To address this, we propose SpecMRL, which reorganizes the feature space to enable more effective prefix readout. Specifically, we extend MRL with a parameter-derived spectral basis organized with feature importance. SpecMRL otherwise follows standard MRL, with prefix readout performed in the learned spectral space. Experiments on MTEB show that SpecMRL outperforms MRL at all evaluated reduced dimensions, with larger gains under more aggressive compression. These results suggest that the learned spectral basis better prioritizes informative directions, enabling more effective representation compression. Notably, SpecMRL extends the performance frontier of MRL, surpassing full-dimensional MRL with only half the embedding dimensions.</p></details> | preprint |
| **[TriFold: Folding irregular 3D Gaussians into structured latents for 3D Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23050v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent progress in 3D generation has motivated growing interest in compact generative representations of complex 3D scenes. For native 3D Gaussian scenes, however, learning such representations remains challenging due to widely varying primitive counts, nonuniform spatial density, and differences in geometry and appearance even among spatially neighboring primitives. In this work, we introduce TriFold, a native 3D Gaussian VAE that encodes Gaussian scenes into a fixed-layout triplane latent and jointly reconstructs their geometry and appearance. The key insight is to impose structural regularity on the latent space while preserving the spatial adaptivity of the underlying Gaussians. During encoding, we use cell-wise learned queries to aggregate projected Gaussians into multiple slots per cell, reducing the information loss caused by single-feature pooling. During decoding, occupancy prediction identifies active regions, where query-based set prediction and candidate selection reconstruct local Gaussian sets with spatially varying primitive counts. Building on this representation, we train a latent flow-matching model for conditional scene-level 3DGS generation, enabling point-cloud-conditioned synthesis and single-image-conditioned amodal generation. Experiments demonstrate that TriFold achieves high-fidelity reconstruction and generation from a single image that provides only a partial observation of the scene, validating its effectiveness for native 3D Gaussian representation learning. https://fantasy-amap.github.io/TriFold</p></details> | <details><summary>30pag...</summary><p>30pages, Project page: https://fantasy-amap.github.io/TriFold</p></details> |
| **[Hierarchical Token Merging with Recursive Autoencoders for Unsupervised Grammar Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26695v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hierarchical abstraction is a fundamental principle across many data modalities, enabling complex sequences to be represented compositionally at multiple levels. Learning such hierarchies directly from data, without explicit structural supervision, remains a central challenge in representation learning. We investigate whether meaningful hierarchical structure can emerge from compression alone. While autoencoders learn compressed representations through reconstruction, this objective does not necessarily favour meaningful compositional structure. We study this challenge through unsupervised grammar induction, where the underlying hierarchy can be evaluated explicitly. We introduce a hierarchical token-merging autoencoder that recursively combines adjacent tokens into increasingly abstract representations, compressing entire sequences through a variational information bottleneck. The model jointly learns representations and hierarchical structure by marginalising over possible binary trees using dynamic programming. To address training instability, we combine independently trained models through unsupervised consensus and distillation. Experiments on a synthetic, ambiguous grammar demonstrate the emergence of meaningful hierarchical structure without structural supervision. The resulting model achieves 93% grammatical parse-validity and 93% exact reconstruction on held-out sequences, compared with 42% parse-validity for the strongest trivial baseline. These findings establish a proof of concept for hierarchical token merging as a mechanism for unsupervised structure discovery and abstraction learning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Global Time-Aware Conditioning (GTC): A Mechanism for Time-Aware Pediatric Sleep Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26618v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sleep physiology evolves throughout the night, with changes in sleep cycles, respiratory stability, and cardiovascular dynamics. | **[BERT Is Already a Denoiser: Cheaply Unlocking Iterative Generation in Masked Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26601v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Masked language models (MLMs) such as BERT are typically used for representation learning despite their denoising pretraining. Frozen BERT already exhibits substantial denoising capability, reconstructing 61.0%, 37.5%, and 24.4% of masked tokens at 15%, 45%, and 60% corruption, respectively, although generation deteriorates sharply as visible context disappears. This capability alone does not yield task-directed generation: frozen BERT reaches only 11.0% on ARC-Easy and 4.2% on MMLU. Ordinary MLM adaptation, however, rapidly converts the same architecture into a usable generator. Full adaptation reaches 28.2% and 24.6%, respectively, while parameter-efficient adaptation substantially reduces unmatched outputs. On open-ended conditional generation, full MLM adaptation reaches BLEU/ROUGE-L of 0.163/0.583 on QQP and 0.107/0.327 on Quasar-T, placing it in the same broad performance range as published BERT models trained through diffusion without explicit diffusion training. | **[Learning Geometry-Aware Planning Representations for Monocular Flight with Cross-Modal Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26508v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing high-speed navigation systems for micro aerial vehicles (MAVs) commonly rely on depth cameras, stereo vision, or LiDAR to obtain explicit geometric information, while monocular cameras offer a lighter and lower-cost alternative. This approach relies on the core assumption that the exemplar image-text pairs constitute two representations of an identical concept. There are two distinct viewpoints on how to address this issue: one suggests mitigating the misalignment, and the other leveraging it. We seek here to reconcile these seemingly opposing perspectives and to provide a practical guide for practitioners. Using latent variable models, we thus formalize cross-modal misalignment by introducing two specific mechanisms: \emph{selection bias}, where some semantic variables are absent in the text, and \emph{perturbation bias}, where semantic variables are altered---both leading to misalignment in data pairs. D.2</p></details> |

This survey synthesises these developments across invasive and non-invasive measurements. We examine model development, public resources and the evolution of evaluation, and compare published performance and communication costs within their reported protocols. The synthesis identifies complementary routes to progress: phonetic, acoustic and semantic targets preserve different aspects of a message; shared representations support reuse across recording conditions and tasks; and online communication increasingly depends on calibration, feedback and user control alongside decoding accuracy. | **[AirMPA: A Meteorology-to-Pollution Adapter for Global Air Quality Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25797v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate real-time forecasting of atmospheric pollutants is essential for reducing exposure risks and improving public health. Under decentralized execution, two key challenges arise: object-dependent representation learning under partial observability and coordination learning in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) under non-stationarity. DeReCo adopts a two-phase training strategy: (1) coordination learning with privileged object information, and (2) transition to decentralized execution through reconstruction of object-dependent representations from local observations and retraining of coordination policies using the reconstructed representations. Experimental results show that DeReCo outperforms baselines in simulation on three training objects, generalizes to six unseen objects with varying masses and friction coefficients under similar grasping configurations, and achieves superior performance on two unseen objects in real-robot experiments.</p></details> | 9 pages, 7 figures |

| **[Language Models Struggle to Use Representations Learned In-Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04212v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Though large language models (LLMs) have enabled great success across a wide variety of tasks, they still appear to fall short of one of the loftier goals of artificial intelligence research: creating an artificial system that can adapt its behavior to radically new contexts upon deployment. Code is released at https://github.com/brightest66/HRIL.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[More Treatments More Problems: Designing the 2026 American Causal Inference Conference Data Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26557v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper documents the design of the 2026 American Causal Inference Conference (ACIC) Data Challenge, which uses a large set of simulations to evaluate the performance of a wide variety of causal inference estimators chosen and implemented by challenge participants. Unlike previous ACIC Data Challenges, which tended to mimic observational studies with a single binary treatment, the 2026 Data Challenge focused on randomized control trials with five arms (one control and four active treatments) to isolate key estimation challenges that arise when simultaneously estimating multiple treatment effects. We created 9,000 distinct synthetic populations corresponding to 18 distinct settings to explore variation in performance corresponding to patterns of treatment effect heterogeneity and response surface specifications. From each population, a sample of n = 1,000 was drawn for participants to estimate individual, subgroup, sample, and population-level treatment effects and identify the optimal treatment arm at each level. We describe several strategies used to generate the data to prevent Challenge participants from exploiting structural sim across datasets and ``gaming the system.'' By detailing these simulation design considerations and data generation choices, this paper provides a framework for conducting large-scale, unconfounded synthetic benchmark studies to rigorously evaluate multi-treatment causal inference procedures.</p></details> |  |
| **[Where to weight? Estimating population causal effects with weighted double score matching in complex surveys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25870v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Double score matching is an emerging method for causal inference that combines propensity and prognostic scores to achieve dimension reduction and model robustness. However, when the observational data are drawn from a complex survey, the existing literature does not provide sufficient guidance on the proper use of survey weights in matching strategies for population-level causal inference. We develop weighted double score matching (WDSM) that provides a unified framework for incorporating survey weights throughout the score estimation, matching, and outcome analysis under both retrospective (informative) and prospective (non-informative) sampling designs. We establish asymptotic normality of the resultant effect estimates under both known and estimated scores and develop a linearization-based bootstrap procedure for variance estimation that preserves the survey-weighted matching structure. Simulation studies show that WDSM substantially reduces bias relative to unweighted matching when either the propensity score model or the prognostic score models are correctly specified. Under correct specification, WDSM also improves efficiency and confidence interval coverage relative to survey-weighted propensity score matching, with particularly pronounced gains under poor overlap. Applications to early marijuana use and mental health in the National Survey on Drug Use and Health and to sleep duration and glycemic control in the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey illustrate the practical value of WDSM for population-level inference. We conclude with a discussion of our findings and provide practical guidance on incorporating survey weights into matching-based analyses of observational data.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 3 figures; 173-page Web Appendix included (198 pages total)</p></details> |
| **[Inverse Confounding Analysis: An Exact Method for Quantifying the Significance of Confounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11991v5)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The presence of unmeasured confounding factors during the collection of observational data may lead to biased estimates of the effect of an exposure on an outcome. Consequently, a central problem in causal inference based on observational data is sensitivity analysis with respect to unmeasured confounding. Existing sensitivity analyses generally focus on worst-case bounds. We propose an exact method for quantifying the significance of confounding, defined here in terms of the complete range of analytical estimates of the stratification-based Risk Ratio over the set of all joint distributions compatible with the observed characteristics. We refer to the proposed method as Inverse Confounding Analysis (ICA). The proposed ICA method extends the widely used E-value approach but, in contrast to it, does not restrict the analysis to a worst-case lower bound. Instead, it provides exact estimates over the entire set of admissible configurations. This requires several additional input parameters, namely the frequencies of the exposure, the confounder, and the outcome. The ICA method is based on an inverse problem: reconstructing the set of admissible joint distributions from specified frequencies and pairwise associations. We formulate this reconstruction problem as a system of nonlinear equations and obtain an analytical solution. Surprisingly, the complete solution set can be parameterized linearly by a single free parameter. The corresponding stratification-based Risk Ratio is then represented as a fractional-linear function of this parameter. This representation makes it possible to derive exact analytical measures of the significance of confounding over the entire set of admissible statistical configurations.</p></details> |  |

While exact analytical bounds exist for certain graphs, this is not generally the case. In this paper, we introduce a framework for estimating such bounds using neural generative models together with a new extrapolation procedure to estimate these bounds more accurately. Across multiple causal inference settings, it produces bounds that are consistently closer to the true bounds than those obtained with existing general neural approaches. | **[A Bayesian Additive Regression Tree Model for Learning Conditional Average Treatment Effects in Regression Discontinuity Designs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.00326v4)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper develops an effective Bayesian approach to conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimation in regression discontinuity designs (RDD), an increasingly prevalent form of quasi-experiment that facilitates causal inference. | **[Which Covariates to Adjust for? Specification-robust Causal Inference in Observational Studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08729v4)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In observational causal inference, domain knowledge often leaves multiple covariate adjustments plausible, yet which of them satisfy ignorability is not testable. | **[What Social Media Teaches Us About Accumulative AI Risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24006v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prominent AI safety discussions emphasize existential threats and catastrophic events, such as AI-enabled nuclear conflict or engineered biological outbreaks. Repeated reassurance, selective exposure, recurring decision biases, and growing personal profiles may gradually narrow perspectives, weaken independent judgment, or enable exploitative influence without a single clearly harmful event. We advance the Trajectory Neglect Thesis: a focus on vivid catastrophic events and identifiable incidents risks overlooking less visible harms that accumulate through the widespread daily use of AI. Social media research motivates this position by showing how reciprocal media effects, social reinforcement, and individual susceptibility shape outcomes beyond individual encounters. We examine how related mechanisms may operate in AI systems, using conversational AI as a concrete setting where generation, semantic inference, and persistent memory may tighten feedback loops. Sycophancy, manipulation, and privacy loss provide a case study of how these risks may interact and accumulate. This comparison identifies mechanisms to investigate rather than assuming that repeated AI use is harmful. We therefore call for evaluation of cumulative patterns of use and deployment, individual and societal outcomes, careful causal inference, and proportionate safeguards that preserve useful assistance and informed user choice.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera-ready version for CogMI 2026 vision track</p></details> |

| **[Graph Dirichlet Energy for Fitting-Free Bivariate Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21892v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose the Graph Dirichlet Energy Causal Inference (GDECI) criterion for distinguishing cause from effect in bivariate settings. The score is the Dirichlet energy of this signal, induced by the unnormalized graph Laplacian. The reverse score swaps the variables, and the lower-energy direction is inferred as causal. The method requires neither regression fitting nor independence testing. After the empirical probability integral transform, its decisions are invariant under strictly monotone transformations of either variable, and the graph operations reduce to convolutions, allowing exact evaluation in $O(n\log n)$ time and $O(n)$ memory. At the population level, GDECI compares degree-weighted Bayes squared prediction errors plus a non-negative smoothness term of the regression function. As the bandwidth tends to zero, the weights become uniform and the smoothness term vanishes, reducing the criterion to a RECI-type comparison of expected conditional variances in copula space. Empirically, we compare GDECI with QCCD, GRCI, CAM, IGCI (two variants), RESIT, LOCI, QPE-k, and RECI (two variants), in accuracy on 1899 cause--effect pairs and in inference speed. On the real-world Tuebingen pairs, it ranked third in unweighted accuracy ($0.684$) and fifth in weighted accuracy ($0.678$). It was also the fastest of the compared methods up to $n\approx10^{4}$. OS-observed file and network effects acquire a semantic tool or agent principal only through an observed process plus a justified command-to-process association; session-correlated file-system events do not by themselves provide that principal. | **[Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.</p></details> | 15 pages, 4 figures |

This tension suggests that safety and general reasoning should not be treated as separate training goals. We instead view safety as an LLM capability that can be learned through a reasoning structure shared with general tasks. Both require the model to understand intent or task goals, apply relevant knowledge, and reason about potential consequences. We introduce safety mid-training, a stage between pre-training and post-training that jointly develops safety and general reasoning through this shared structure. Given safety and utility prompts, the base model generates structured documents and reasoning traces that are then used for mid-training. Safety mid-training reduces average attack success rate (ASR) by 72% to 81% relative to Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B base models, while also improving general capability. The benefits persist through subsequent post-training. After general-only SFT, mid-trained models largely retain their safety, achieving lower ASR than the baselines without sacrificing general capability. They also retain safety under general-only GRPO without safety rewards, while subsequent RL reaches a better safety-utility frontier when initialized from the mid-trained models. Together, these results suggest that learning safety and general reasoning within a shared mid-training stage can reduce the safety-utility trade-off and make safety more robust to subsequent training.</p></details> |  |

Over 148 feeding days, three models kept the 90% confidence interval of their mean error within a pre-set 10% margin, but no model reached the pre-set 30% limits of agreement. Protein was under-estimated by every model, most in protein-dense meals; repeated calls changed error by at most 1.4 points; a high reasoning effort helped only the smallest model (4.2 points) and changed the others by less than 1 point. A low average bias is not evidence of accurate individual intake estimation; such estimates should support, not replace, assessment by a dietitian.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 1 figure, 12 tables. Code, prompts, pre-registration and per-meal outputs: https://github.com/xtea/llm-intake-validation</p></details> |

Meaning-preserving paraphrases of the input yield the largest accuracy gains (+1.80 pp over a single call) and reach peak accuracy with the fewest samples. Yet, they are not the most diverse: character-level noise makes the responses disagree far more often (37.8% versus 27.4%) while gaining less accuracy, and perturbing two modalities at once falls below the single-call baseline. Not all diversity is equally useful: perturbations that keep the meaning intact produce disagreement that aggregation can use, while those that corrupt it produce disagreement that is wrong. Overall, our results suggest that for mid-tier LLMs such as Claude Haiku, varying how a question is phrased is a better use of an inference call than varying the reasoning path. | **[Accelerated Drift of Scientific Prose in the LLM Era: Evidence from an Interrupted Time-Series Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26008v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every scientific text is embedded in the writing norms of its time: it reflects earlier publications while also shaping the texts that follow. We investigate whether the trajectories of scientific-preprint writing changed in rate or direction after conversational LLMs became publicly accessible, using the release of ChatGPT in November 2022 as the observational breakpoint. We compare pre- and post-2022 trajectories across vocabulary, punctuation, readability, and syntax in abstracts from arXiv's artificial intelligence, statistics, and quantitative biology categories and from medRxiv. Using interrupted time-series regression, we find substantial post-2022 changes across multiple feature families and research domains, including increases in average word length, reduced formula-based readability, and declines in maximum dependency-tree depth and sentence-level depth variation. Individual features follow different dynamics: some lexical markers accelerate persistently, while others rise and then recede. Our findings show that isolated marker frequencies capture only part of the shift: changes in the rate, direction, and structure of linguistic trajectories provide complementary evidence of broader post-2022 change in scientific-preprint writing. More broadly, these findings motivate monitoring scientific writing as a multidimensional, evolving system rather than through a fixed set of LLM-associated markers.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EMNLP 2026. 22 pages, including references and appendices</p></details> |
| **[Calibrating LLM Judges for Human and AI Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29431v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Measuring how successful a conversation is remains difficult, even for humans judging spoken dialogue. We further release the Voice Arena Goal Dataset (VA), 200 task-oriented human-AI and human-agent conversations with pairwise annotations, revealing a substantial gap between current judges and human-level discrimination. Using VA, we test whether CANDOR-fitted calibration transfers to human-AI conversations, finding it brings judges onto a shared scale despite never observing VA during fitting.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scaling Synthetic Pre-training Data and Evaluation for Indic LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26006v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Building capable language models for India requires scalable multilingual data and robust evaluation. Includes appendices and references</p></details> |
| **[LLM as a Tool, Not an Agent: Code-Mined Tree Transformations for Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16555v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Neural Architecture Search (NAS) aims to automatically discover high-performing deep neural network (DNN) architectures. At each evolution step, coarse-level planning is governed by a diversity-guided algorithm that leverages Bayesian modeling to improve exploration efficiency, while the LLM resolves the remaining degrees of freedom to ensure a meaningful evolutionary trajectory and an executable generated architecture. With this formulation, instead of fully agentic LLM approaches, our method explores diverse directions beyond the inherent biases in the LLM. Our method improves over existing NAS methods by 0.69, 1.83, and 2.68 points on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet16-120, demonstrating its effectiveness.</p></details> | 58 pages |
| **[Auditor-Assisted Summary-Channel Verification for Hosted LLM Identity Substitution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25982v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hosted LLM services increasingly expose model identity as a product claim, but external users and auditors often cannot inspect which model configuration served a request. Across model families, scales, and task domains, SUMMARK reliably detects cross-family and cross-scale substitutions at low false-positive rates while preserving task-facing answer quality. We further study FP16->INT8/INT4 precision-consistency diagnostics and robustness under bounded post-processing, rewriting stress tests, and text-only spoofing, revealing both strong performance and clear failure boundaries. | **[Behavioral Probes for Information Flow in LLM Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25966v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Most existing approaches to LLM swarm optimization are utility-driven, focusing on final task utility while leaving the processing, integration, and reliance of information within individual interactions largely implicit. Specifically, we repurpose External Context Score (ECS) and Parametric Knowledge Score (PKS)---originally designed for hallucination analysis---to measure a node's external-context dependence and parametric-knowledge dependence, respectively. Across controlled and realistic swarm settings, we observe consistent patterns: ECS increases with input relevance and distinguishes useful multi-source contributions, while PKS decreases as informative context accumulates. Building on this heuristic interpretation, we demonstrate how these probes can complement utility-driven structure optimization in two controlled applications. Probe-guided node pruning reduces redundant communication and token consumption, and probe-guided reasoning-path search accelerates convergence compared to utility-only baselines. Our empirical results suggest that behavioral probes can make the internal information flow of LLM swarms observable and serve as effective auxiliary signals for structure optimization.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Revisiting Cauchy-Schwarz Norm Bounds for SDC Detection in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18557v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) execute billions of floating-point operations per output token across many hardware accelerators. At this scale, hardware-induced faults can bypass system-level detection mechanisms and cause Silent Data Corruption (SDC). Algorithm-Based Fault Tolerance (ABFT) is the state-of-the-art technique for protecting matrix multiplication from such faults, but it incurs non-negligible computational overhead and requires thresholds to distinguish hardware-induced errors from floating-point roundoff. In this paper, we revisit Cauchy--Schwarz Norm Bounds (CSNB) as a low-overhead alternative for detecting SDC during LLM inference. We compare CSNB against ABFT through controlled fault injection and evaluate the runtime overhead of both verification and threshold determination across multiple model sizes, batch sizes, and generation lengths. Our results show that, on average, CSNB retains 99% of the fault detection capability of ABFT for the evaluated linear layers in dense LLMs, while reducing verification overhead by 87% and threshold determination overhead by 67%. For Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) router gate computations, however, CSNB retains only 55% of the fault detection capability of ABFT. These findings suggest that CSNB is a promising low-overhead alternative for protecting linear layers in dense LLMs, while checksum-based verification remains preferable for the evaluated MoE router gate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Productive State-Space Exploration for Multi-turn RL in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25957v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Model (LLM) agents in multi-turn, long-horizon environments remains challenging due to inefficient action selection and repetitive behavioral patterns. In particular, agents often become trapped in high-confidence yet unproductive behaviors, repeatedly executing invalid actions or entering action loops that hinder task completion. We observe that these frequently violate environment preconditions, revealing a mismatch between the agent's representation of the internal state and the dynamics of the underlying environment. In addition, repetitive high-confidence actions lead to cyclical behaviors that provide little to no progress towards the goal. We argue that addressing these challenges requires more productive task-aligned exploration to improve the agent's understanding of environment dynamics. Motivated by intrinsic curiosity-driven exploration, we propose a conditional state-space exploration framework that balances task completion with intrinsic information-seeking behavior. Specifically, we introduce an intrinsic reward that penalizes actions whose observation duplicates prior state memory. This provides the benefit of reducing redundant behaviors and encouraging state-space coverage without relying on a general information-theoretic measure. Strong experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially improves policy learning in multi-turn environments, mitigating invalid actions and repetitive loops.</p></details> |  |

We introduce cxbench, a benchmark that evaluates outcome and process compliance separately across six regulated domains. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. A hybrid grader combines deterministic checks for ordering and authority with an LLM judge for semantic requirements. We define a false-pass as an outcome-pass run that fails process checks. Across seven models and 120 tasks, 55.5% of the 840 retained executions are false-passes, representing 80.1% of outcome-passing runs; only 13.8% pass both checks. Process-inclusive evaluation changes the observed model ordering and exposes differences hidden by outcome-only scoring. cxbench makes these failures inspectable through trace-linked criteria.</p></details> |  |
| **[AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25950v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. | **[Keep the Lineages Alive: Diversity-Preserving SMC for Diffusion LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25932v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inference-time sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) decoding can improve the reasoning accuracy of masked diffusion language models (diffusion LLMs) without post-training by resampling a population of partial generations according to a reward. Its effectiveness therefore depends on the resampling step, which must concentrate computation on promising candidates while retaining enough diversity to explore alternative reasoning paths. The reward can be internal, the decoder's own confidence on the committed prefix, or external, a process reward model scoring that prefix. Under either reward, multinomial resampling can disrupt this balance by duplicating high-weight particles and discarding lineages that may still reach a correct solution. The effective sample size (ESS) does not register this loss: multinomial resampling resets it to its maximum even as distinct lineages disappear. To retain such lineages, we introduce lineage-preserving SMC (LP-SMC), which combines Chopthin resampling with an input weight floor. Instead of equalizing weights, Chopthin bounds the ratio between the largest and smallest weights it returns and carries the unequal weights forward. The floor limits how far below the maximum a weight may fall before resampling so that lineages with low early weight keep a chance to recover. Across mathematical-reasoning and code-generation benchmarks on two base models, LP-SMC raises oracle coverage, the fraction of problems with at least one correct particle, in every setting, by 1.8 to 25.6 points, and raises end-to-end accuracy under a common reward-model selector in eleven of twelve settings, by up to 7.9 points on LLaDA-1.5 and 18.3 on Dream-7B. The coverage gain persists under the external reward model, which suggests that diversity-preserving resampling and the guidance signal contribute separately to inference-time search.</p></details> |  |

On the other hand, classical search algorithms have the potential to solve the planning problems perfectly, yet they require fully observable environments, known state transitions and goal state, which are often unattainable in general task domains. Therefore, we propose to implement an LLM-guided search algorithm that makes the search process possible and efficient by incorporating LLMs' commonsense knowledge. Our method leverages LLMs to (1) translate natural language instructions into formatted goal conditions, (2) facilitate efficient exploration in partially observable environments, (3) implement a rule-based world simulator, and (4) prune the space of candidate actions to improve search efficiency. Experimental results on VirtualHome and ALFWorld demonstrate its superior performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>EMNLP...</summary><p>EMNLP 2026 findings. Code will be released at https://github.com/woyut/TESS_EMNLP26</p></details> |
| **[Budgeted Attack Scheduling for LLM Red-Teaming: The Roles of Static Priors, Semantic Guidance, and Online Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25897v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As jailbreak attacks against large language models (LLMs) become increasingly diverse, red-teaming has come to rely on large libraries of jailbreak prompts and attack strategies to evaluate the robustness of victim models and their defense settings. However, attack effectiveness is highly condition-dependent: an attack that succeeds under one victim-defense setting may fail under another, while exhaustively evaluating the full attack library for each setting incurs substantial token and monetary costs. This motivates studying how jailbreak attacks should be prioritized under a limited attempt budget for efficient robustness evaluation. In this work, we formalize this problem as budgeted attack scheduling over a fixed attack library and investigate three complementary signals under a fixed victim-defense condition: (1) static historical attack success rate (ASR) priors; (2) semantic guidance from LLMs; and (3) online feedback from earlier queries. Building on these signals, we design four scheduling strategies and evaluate them across three victim models and three defense settings on GPT-5.2. Extensive experiments clearly characterize the conditions under which each signal is most effective. Historical ASR provides a strong initialization, but its reliability degrades under defense-induced distribution shifts. Semantic guidance consistently improves or matches static prior-based scheduling in the cold-start regime, while online feedback becomes particularly valuable when transferred priors become stale. Semantic guidance and online feedback can also partially compensate for missing historical priors. Taken together, these findings characterize budgeted red-teaming as an attack scheduling problem in which historical priors, semantic guidance, and online feedback play complementary roles.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[Learning Who Talks to Whom: Role-Aware Communication Topology Scheduling for Efficient LLM Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25851v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems driven by large language models (LLMs) rely on structured communication topologies to facilitate information exchange and collaborative reasoning. However, effectively scheduling these topologies to dynamically determine who talks to whom and when to communicate remains a fundamental challenge in balancing collective reasoning performance against exponential token overhead. Existing topology scheduling approaches either enforce static communication graphs that cannot adapt to dynamic problem-solving states, or overlook agent role complementarity while failing to explicitly incorporate the synthesized graph structure into team-level decision evaluation. To tackle these limitations, we propose ACTS, a role-aware adaptive communication topology scheduling framework for efficient LLM multi-agent collaboration. Orchestrated within a Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE) paradigm, ACTS introduces a novel dual-attention mechanism. Specifically, a Role-conditioned Relational Attention module dynamically selects variable-cardinality interaction partners based on agent roles and evolving execution states, while a Topological Attention Mixer explicitly embeds the directed communication topology into team value decomposition for precise credit assignment. Comprehensive evaluations across 9 challenging benchmarks covering code generation, multi-step reasoning, general knowledge, and competitive mathematics demonstrate that ACTS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, boosting average accuracy by up to 6.19 percentage points while reducing token consumption by over 15%. | **[AURA-Eval: Evaluation Framework for Acting Under Risk Awareness in LLM Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06783v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly taking actions with real consequences, yet safety evaluations often reduce their behavior to a final safe/unsafe outcome, obscuring whether the agent recognized the risk and how it responded. We introduce AURA-Eval, a framework for evaluating whether agents recognize contextual risk and adapt their behavior accordingly. AURA-Eval truncates tool-use trajectories at safety-critical decision points and asks the evaluated model to generate the next thought and action. It creates controlled scenario variations along six risk mechanisms: harm intensity, scale, target susceptibility, system dependency, oversight, and reversibility. It also creates paired scenarios that preserve the underlying goal but differ in whether safe fulfillment is available, allowing unsafe compliance and over-refusal to be evaluated together. An LLM-judge panel, validated against human annotations, evaluates each continuation along three axes: risk detection from the reasoning trace, and action type and safety from the action. From 157 R-Judge seed trajectories, we construct 1,249 evaluation items and evaluate 20 frontier, open-weight, and safety-tuned models. Across all 20 models, unsafe-action rates are higher when no safe path for request fulfillment is available than when one is; for frontier models, the rates are 27-40% versus 5-7%. When a safe path exists, frontier models rarely refuse; when none exists, they most often propose an alternative. Open-weight models, by contrast, more often execute the request than propose alternatives and act unsafely on 58-83% of items. Controlled risk-mechanism analyses further show particularly high unsafe rates under changes to oversight and scale; removing pre-execution oversight flips 52.3% of cases that were safe on the corresponding original scenario to unsafe.</p></details> |  |
| **[When to Stop Asking: Factor Coverage Bias and LLM Determinability Judgments with Underspecified Information in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25819v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical decision-making often begins with underspecified information and requires not only gathering missing information but also knowing when to stop. This calls for judging determinability (whether the available information is sufficient for a decision) by considering whether missing information could change the conclusion, not simply the amount of available decision-relevant information. Reliance on this amount as a cue for determinability, which we term \textit{factor coverage bias}, could undermine the trustworthiness of large language models (LLMs) through premature conclusions or excessive information gathering, such as unnecessary invasive tests. Yet whether LLMs exhibit this bias remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we construct a controlled pairwise benchmark in which LLMs identify the determinable case within each determinable--undeterminable pair. We add a decision-relevant factor to either side while keeping each case's determinability unchanged, and similarly add irrelevant text to distinguish this bias from context-length effects. Adding a relevant factor made LLMs more likely to select the augmented case as determinable even when it remained undeterminable, an effect that persisted with increased reasoning effort, while adding irrelevant text made them less likely to select the augmented case, an effect that reasoning substantially reduced. These findings identify factor coverage bias as a failure mode distinct from context-length effects and a potential risk to LLM trustworthiness. Our work provides an evaluation framework for LLM trustworthiness under underspecification.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 8 figures, 8 tables</p></details> |

Using I-CAN-Debias, we find that self-debiasing neurons account for less than 2% of model parameters yet are causally critical: deactivating them raises the biased-response rate above 95%. These neurons cluster in the query and value projections of later layers and exhibit highly asymmetric cross-category transferability. Furthermore, targeted fine-tuning of these neurons substantially improves fairness while preserving general capabilities. Importantly, models tuned with I-CAN-Debias generalize robustly across diverse evaluations beyond the training distribution, spanning open-ended fairness, jailbreak attacks, and implicit bias, without requiring test-time debiasing prompts. | **[Service-Induced Congestion in Memory-Constrained LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15555v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In large language model (LLM) serving, each request accumulates persistent graphics processing unit (GPU) memory during service as its key-value cache grows with every generated token. Under high concurrency, aggregate memory usage therefore increases endogenously over time: the service process itself creates future capacity pressure. When memory capacity is exceeded, systems evict active requests, discarding cached state and restarting them later, which wastes computation and reduces throughput. We develop a discrete-time dynamical model of memory-constrained LLM inference that captures admission, memory growth, and eviction under continuous batching. In the saturated-input regime, the system admits both eviction-free fixed points and limit cycles with evictions. For homogeneous workloads, we show that the eviction-free equilibrium is unstable and that, except for a Lebesgue-measure-zero exact-capture set, the system converges to a unique worst-case limit cycle that is asymptotically stable outside this exceptional set, with throughput losses as large as 50%. For heterogeneous workloads, we prove a stability criterion in the two-class common-input setting and explain how the survival-polynomial mechanism generalizes to multiple classes and heterogeneous-input lengths. Under an input-dominated scaling regime, coprime decoding lengths stabilize the eviction-free equilibrium, while non-coprime lengths create synchronized modes that drive instability. These results characterize when workload heterogeneity desynchronizes completions and helps stabilize memory-constrained serving. More broadly, we identify service-induced congestion as a structural instability mechanism and derive scheduling design principles for sustaining high throughput.</p></details> | <details><summary>100 p...</summary><p>100 pages. v2: adds GPU experiments with benchmark workloads (Appendix D.4) and revises the figures</p></details> |
| **[How Much Does One Wrong Answer Cost? Error Propagation in Constraint Pruning with Noisy LLM Oracles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25739v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Constraint pruning saves queries to a large language model (LLM) or another noisy oracle by skipping each yes/no question implied by answers accepted earlier, for example through transitivity. Entity-resolution systems and LLM-based rankers use this idea. However, their analyses typically assume a correct oracle. We argue that pruning should be judged by its risk when the oracle errs, since an accepted wrong answer spreads to every question it implies. When each answer is wrong independently with small probability $\varepsilon$, the first-order risk is set by how many facts one wrong answer corrupts on average. We consider two kinds of constraints. For implication rules, as in taxonomies, pruning in a random order adds no first-order risk: a wrong answer that would change $k$ facts is accepted with probability $1/k$, so it costs one fact on average. Rules with $s$ premises break this balance: a wrong answer at a premise shared by $r$ rules can cost order $r^{1-1/s}$ facts. For entity resolution with $m$ records of one entity, the first-order risk equals $\varepsilon$ times the Wiener index (the total pairwise distance) of the spanning tree built by the query order. Pivot orders, which first compare one record with all others, amplify errors by less than 2 and are optimal; random orders by order $m^{1/3}$; the common most-similar-first order is the worst in one dimension and, for large $m$, worse than random in two. With several entities, the cost of wrongly merging two does not depend on how many others exist. Simulations agree with every predicted constant within sampling error.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, including appendices</p></details> |

Static structured pruning is hardware-friendly, but its input-agnostic computation allocation often degrades model quality at high sparsity. Dynamic pruning adapts computation to individual tokens, but coarse-grained layer-level routing limits allocation flexibility, and finer-grained decisions introduce irregular execution that can offset computational savings. To address these challenges, we present WIDE, the first end-to-end differentiable framework that combines token-wise structured dynamic width pruning with GPU kernel co-design for both prefill and decoding. WIDE uses differentiable routers to select GQA-aligned query-head groups and GPU-tile-aligned FFN-channel groups for each token. To execute these decisions efficiently, it reorders routing masks to cluster active tokens within each group, enabling hardware-agnostic block-level skipping and architecture-aware skipping of inactive loads and tensor-core operations. Experiments on the Llama3 and Qwen3 families demonstrate improved quality retention over representative static and dynamic pruning methods. At 50\% target routing sparsity, WIDE improves Llama3.1-8B average zero-shot accuracy by up to 20.26 points over the strongest dynamic-depth baseline under calibration-only settings, while retaining 97.6\%/88.2\% of dense Qwen3-14B likelihood/generation performance after LoRA tuning. | **[MOSAIC: Multi-Objective Search for Allocating Inference Cache Across LLM Decoder Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25431v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Key-Value (KV) cache is the main memory bottleneck of long-context LLM inference. | **[VReAct: Verification as an Architectural Layer for LLM Agents: A V-Model Design, and a Pilot Study of Its Deterministic Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31937v3)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents built on the ReAct pattern concentrate four responsibilities in one model: selecting a strategy, choosing each action, formatting it, and judging whether the result is adequate. Such raw trajectory tokens may not explicitly capture clinical semantics that clinicians use in practice, such as patient severity, recent treatment response, and current risk priorities. To address this limitation, we propose ClinSDT, an LLM-assisted Decision Transformer framework for offline sepsis treatment. ClinSDT uses a frozen LLM as a semantic bridge rather than a treatment decision maker. It constructs three clinical semantic views from ICU trajectories, namely Profile, Context, and Advisory, and inserts their encoded representations as independent tokens into a Decision Transformer policy. To support policy refinement and rollout, ClinSDT further trains a Treatment-Response Forecaster to estimate the next physiological state, SAPS-II change, and predictive uncertainty from state-action inputs. In the second training stage, the forecaster screens local counterfactual treatment candidates, and only low-uncertainty candidates with lower predicted SAPS-II increase are distilled into the policy after semantic regeneration. During inference, ClinSDT performs closed-loop treatment rollout by refreshing semantic inputs from the currently available patient state. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show that ClinSDT achieves consistent relative improvements over representative imitation learning, offline reinforcement learning, and Decision Transformer baselines under the same rollout protocol. Code and data are available at https://github.com/KAG778/ClinSDT.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 6 figures. Accepted at CIKM 2026 (Full Research Track, Oral Presentation)</p></details> |
| **[Memory as Belief: Bayesian Evolution of Memory for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25119v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to accumulate past experiences for continual adaptation. However, effectively reusing memory requires accurately assessing its value, as retrieved experiences may become unreliable when transferred across task contexts. Despite its importance, memory valuation remains largely underexplored, with existing methods relying on historical retrieval success rates that overlook the context dependency of memory usefulness and the varying reliability of estimates under different amounts of evidence. Inspired by the Bayesian brain hypothesis, we propose BEAM (Bayesian-style Evolutionary Agentic Memory), a framework that reformulates memory value evaluation as a parameterized cognitive inference process, where memory value is represented as a context-conditioned dynamic belief rather than a passive historical statistic. Specifically, BEAM infers memory value beliefs from current task contexts and continuously calibrates them using execution feedback as empirical evidence, transforming memory from passive storage into an adaptive cognitive mechanism for continual self-evolution. Extensive experiments across reasoning and code generation benchmarks demonstrate that BEAM consistently improves memory utilization and enables continual self-evolution of LLM agents.</p></details> |  |

| **[Manga2Animate: An Agentic Framework for Faithful Manga Animation with Script and Storyboard Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26851v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adapting existing manga into coherent animation that stays faithful to the source is a valuable yet challenging task. Existing methods either access the manga mainly through text, losing its characters and art style, or feed manga pages directly into video generation models, which misinterpret the manga's structure and produce incorrect storylines. In this paper, we propose Manga2Animate, a multi-agent framework that grounds every stage of generation in the source manga. We decompose the task into two intermediate representations, an animation script and a visual storyboard. Each representation is produced by a creator agent and reviewed against the source panels by a paired reviewer agent. We tailor representation-specific selection and dual-level feedback to progressively refine both before synthesizing the final animation. Extensive experiments and a user study demonstrate that Manga2Animate produces animations more faithful to the source manga and more consistent across shots than state-of-the-art approaches. Project page: https://annchenn.github.io/Manga2Animate/</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://annchenn.github.io/Manga2Animate/</p></details> |
| **[WeAct: Dynamic Weight Switching as Agent Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26847v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 14 figures, 12 tables, including references and appendices</p></details> |
| **[Train An Imaginative Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26845v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A seemingly reasonable push in Sokoban can make the puzzle unsolvable. To avoid such mistakes, an agent has to anticipate the consequences of its actions before execution, especially when feedback from the real environment is sparse or actions are irreversible. We introduce Agentic Imagination (Agima), a training framework that trains an agent to imagine before acting, i.e. to be imaginative. Agima trains a single omni model to propose actions, generate images of their outcomes, and then reason over these imagined futures to decide what to execute in the real environment. Rather than committing to a single plan, the agent can simulate a sequence of actions, explore alternatives, and compare possible futures. We first use supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to unify policy and world modeling within a single omni model. This enables the agent to predict future outcomes and perform basic forms of imagination, such as forward thinking and counterfactual branching. We then use agentic reinforcement learning (RL) to teach the agent to reason with its imagined futures and use them effectively for planning. Across four environments spanning Maze2D, Sokoban, Pacman, and PushT, Agima generalizes to harder settings unseen during training. Trained with SFT alone on two-box Sokoban, Agima solves 40% of four-box puzzles while seeing the real board only once every five moves, compared with an action-only planner that only solves 2%. After RL, new behaviors emerge in how Agima uses its imagination: it learns a new unified format that was unseen during the SFT stage, learns to conduct forward thinking only when necessary, and branches more frequently at critical decision points. An agent that can picture where its actions lead can learn when and where to look ahead, that is, to plan. Project page: https://agima.net/.</p></details> |  |
| **[Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.</p></details> | 15 pages, 4 figures |
| **[AGR: Agentic Generative Retrieval with Tool-Augmented Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26831v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large-scale search and recommendation systems derive much of their retrieval quality from rich, heterogeneous features, yet existing generative retrieval (GR) methods struggle to exploit such feature ecosystems efficiently. Static feature injection either encodes all available signals or places them according to predetermined schemes, increasing serving cost and introducing irrelevant or conflicting evidence. More fundamentally, existing methods cannot adaptively determine when a feature is needed, which feature to select, or where it should influence hierarchical Semantic ID (SID) generation. We propose Agentic Generative Retrieval (AGR), a tool-augmented paradigm that interleaves SID generation with on-demand feature acquisition. Conditioned on the request and decoded SID prefix, AGR decides whether to invoke a tool, which feature to acquire, and where its observation should influence subsequent decoding, thereby steering generation toward feature-consistent SID branches. To supervise this behavior, we construct tool-augmented trajectories based on feature applicability and information gain along the SID hierarchy, and train the model through SID pretraining followed by tool-augmented fine-tuning. Experiments on an industrial dataset and three public benchmarks, together with an online A/B test, consistently demonstrate the effectiveness of AGR.</p></details> |  |
| **[ProteinGeoBench: Frontier Structural-Biology Agents Are Fluent at Lookup but Lost in Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26829v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier agents retrieve structural metadata well and compute geometry from coordinates less reliably. We ask how much of a benchmark score reflects that gap and how much reflects the evaluation configuration around the model. We study ProteinGeoBench, a suite of 131 protein-structure tasks, using 4,249 rollouts from four frontier models and 4,486 further trials across 26 models. Three of the four frontier models fall within 0.031 of one another (0.785 to 0.816) with no difference between them resolved, while the fourth scores 0.707 and all three gaps to it exclude zero. Three of the four do better on metadata lookup than on numeric calculation. The fourth is slightly better on numeric. Numeric tasks are 28 of the 131 but half of the twelve tasks that all four models fail. The ranking also depends on the evaluation budget: with one run per task, 23.9% of bootstrap replicates put a different model first. On a selected 15-task slice, removing network access lowers scores more than blocking eight structural-database domains, but the two contrasts were graded in different passes and cannot be cleanly decomposed. Swapping in each model's own command-line agent changes scores by -0.071 to +0.086, a comparison too small to rule out moderate harness effects. Across 26 models, numeric performance rises with non-numeric performance rather than staying flat. The experiments use different task subsets, so they cannot rank model, harness, access and task composition against one another. Evaluations should report results by task family, along with the number of runs, the harness and the access conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 2 figures, 10 tables. Benchmark and tasks: https://github.com/Protege-AI/ProteinGeoBench</p></details> |
| **[Agent World Model in the Wild: Computer-Use Agent Environment Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26828v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent learning is shifting from learning from human data toward learning through interaction with the world. However, scaling such learning directly in real-world computer systems is challenging and can introduce substantial security risks. We therefore propose Agent World Model in the Wild (WildAWM), a scalable pipeline for synthesizing controllable environments and tasks for training general computer-use agents (CUAs). Whereas existing approaches typically specialize in a single agent interaction interface, we construct executable environments that jointly support graphical user interfaces, model context protocol services, and command-line interfaces, allowing agents to flexibly combine visual interaction, tool use, and code execution. WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. Beyond environments, we address three recurring challenges in task synthesis: ensuring solvability, maintaining sufficient difficulty, and producing reliable verifiers, by introducing execution-based quality gates that combine heterogeneous agent rollouts, cross-judge consistency checks, and counterfactual probes of judgment robustness. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. Both models achieve substantial gains across 8 out-of-distribution benchmarks spanning web, claw, and computer-use agents, demonstrating that learning in WildAWM's synthesized worlds generalizes broadly to unseen computer-use tasks. | **[RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26826v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. | **[Accountable partial understanding: securing the epistemic attack surface of agentic systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26799v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Attackers profit from what defenders do not understand. Software security has treated the gap between system behavior and human comprehension as a condition to narrow through analysis and assurance, booking the remainder as accepted risk. Agentic AI systems change that gap: when software reads, writes, explains, and authorizes security-relevant behavior, partial understanding moves faster, grows more opaque, and couples to delegated authority. This paper characterizes the resulting epistemic attack surface and presents a taxonomy of four interacting attack categories, which target the interpretation of intent, the production and consumption of evidence, the scope of delegated authority, and the comprehension of human supervisors. It then defines accountable partial understanding (APU) and specifies six necessary conditions: bounded scope with explicit assumptions, inspectable evidence, assigned responsibility, visible residual unknowns, actionability, and temporal maintenance. It explains why delegating interpretation to software agents differs from distributing knowledge among people and distinguishes APU from risk acceptance, explainable AI, assurance cases, formal verification, and human-in-the-loop oversight. From the conditions, the paper derives five design principles: bounded delegation, decoupled evidence, assumption tracking with invalidation, role-specific accountability views, and intervention-preserving design with residual unknown registers. It introduces two measurable probe dimensions, evidence linkage and invalidation latency, for testing whether accountability mechanisms hold under change and adversarial pressure. Three running examples (an AI coding agent, an autonomous cyber-defense agent, and an AI-mediated insulin delivery update) ground the analysis. Security without full understanding is already here; the task is to make it accountable.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 1 table. Under review</p></details> |
| **[Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Market Making on Prediction-Market Order Books](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26789v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A market maker provides liquidity by quoting prices to buy and sell an asset, earning the spread while managing inventory risk.
