Three challenges underpin this research: adverse selection may cause a policy trained on real data to stop quoting; the consequences of a quote can arrive many steps after the action; and prediction-market L2 data does not identify individual orders needed for simulation. We address these challenges by reconstructing order-level LOBSTER-format data from Polymarket and training agents in JaxMARL-HFT on 417 real markets. We enrich the baseline market maker's observation space with the book and recent flow, redesign its actions and inventory controls, and propose a cooperative multi-agent decomposition into a quoter and a risk manager trained using recurrent Independent Proximal Policy Optimisation (IPPO) with RNN policies. We investigate reward shaping, particularly reward blending and post-fill markout, to mitigate credit-assignment difficulties between the agents. Our redesigned single-agent market maker quotes more frequently and incurs smaller held-out losses than the retrained JaxMARL-HFT baseline, although the contributions of jointly changed observations, actions and risk constraints are not separately identified. Decomposition alone performs poorly; careful reward design substantially improves the desk relative to the unshaped split and yields our best result. Models are evaluated on realised end-of-episode portfolio value on held-out markets. Extended version of a paper accepted at ICAIF 2026 Workshop on AI-Driven Market Microstructure. MSc dissertation, London School of Economics and Political Science (2026)</p></details> |
| **[Topological Sheaves of Irreducibility: A Theoretical Framework for Multi-Agent Task Obstructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26777v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems can differ under identical agents and communication budgets when they support different forms of joint interaction. A task is represented by an interaction complex $K$ and a capacity sheaf $\mathcal{F}$; nonzero $H^n(K;\mathcal{F})$ defines a structural signature, while registered interventions test capability. For cocycle data, cochain realization is solvable exactly when the represented cohomology class vanishes. Across 15,625 degree-1 inputs, an all-agent additive primitive solves decision and repair exactly; the optimal pairwise system reaches 88.80% on decision, one input above the 88.79% majority-class baseline, and 26.63% on repair. On a degree-2 tetrahedral-boundary task over 531,441 inputs, the same-2-skeleton system retains all four three-agent primitives yet matches the pairwise-only optimum throughout the registered separation window, whereas adding the class-filling four-agent primitive makes both tasks exact. Using the same procedures across tasks lets us directly compare different planning capabilities. For generation, STEPEVAL identifies critical failures that prevent goal completion. | **[Which Model for Which Role? Cost-Efficient Pareto Recommendation for LLM Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26676v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.</p></details> | <details><summary>Preli...</summary><p>Preliminary version accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Resource-Aware Agentic AI (RAAAI)</p></details> |
| **[PACT: Plan-Augmented Coordination via Trace-Graphs for Dynamic Multi-Agent Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26671v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent approaches have been investigated recently as a means of improving the scalability of reinforcement learning for neural combinatorial optimization. Existing methods assume that all problem instance information is known upfront. However, real-world problems are often dynamic: relevant information is progressively revealed as the agents take actions (e.g., arriving at an edge in a routing problem only to discover it is untraversable). In such cases, existing methods fail to coordinate agents to explore and exploit the progressively revealed information. To address the arising coordination challenge, we introduce PACT (Plan-Augmented Coordination via Trace-graphs). PACT augments the environment graph with auxiliary nodes representing the plans of agents, which are connected to the resource nodes to indicate future dependencies. The proposed message-passing scheme over the augmented graph grounds inter-agent coordination in the environment and enables proactive, rather than reactive, conflict resolution. We show that PACT improves solution quality in dynamic problems relative to baselines and exhibits the best transfer to larger instances on which, remarkably, it outperforms centralized oracles with privileged access to instance uncertainty.</p></details> |  |
| **[Honeypots for AI Agent Insider Threats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26664v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents deployed inside organizations can pose an insider threat if they make mistakes, are compromised by an attacker, or pursue misaligned objectives. Rather than only using honeypots for pre-deployment evaluations, defenders should plant decoy opportunities for harmful actions throughout the production environment to detect misbehavior, incriminate malicious agents, and deter attacks. Such honeypots can elicit misbehavior under real deployment conditions, identify malicious agents even if chain-of-thought monitorability degrades, provide reliable warnings about agent misbehavior to security teams, and scale cheaply to many agent instances. The presence of honeypots also introduces uncertainty about which opportunities are real, thus deterring malicious agents from attacking and reducing the rate of misbehavior. We discuss the limitations of honeypots and give reasons to expect them to remain effective as agent capabilities increase. Finally, we outline key research questions to measure and improve the effectiveness of honeypots: testing the robustness of honeypots, comparing their detection effectiveness with other monitoring methods, understanding their incrimination and deterrence effects, and building supporting infrastructure.</p></details> |  |
| **[AllPath: Layered Verification for a Deployed, Self-Revising LLM Trading Agent under Human-Gated and Autonomous Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26662v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM trading agents are evaluated almost exclusively in backtests and autonomous benchmark harnesses. Deployment, where actions are irreversible, raises a different question: who verifies the agent's actions, and which failures can each verifier structurally catch? We report on AllPath, a personal LLM trading agent deployed against a live paper-brokerage account. The system watches positions through a deterministic rules engine, and each night it reviews its own day and proposes revisions to its own trading strategies. Verification is split across three layers. Code verifies what must not depend on judgment: deterministic gates enforce risk limits and block any strategy revision that fails an integrity check. The agent verifies itself within fixed bounds: its nightly review has an iteration cap and no tools that can act. A human verifies judgment, through approval screens that show the current state of each proposal. We also compared two verification policies for the agent's self-revisions on the live system: two and a half weeks with every self-revision human-gated, then five weeks with reflection revisions auto-applied behind the same code gates, while the operator's chat channel stayed human-gated throughout. Each layer caught a different kind of failure. The code gates rejected no proposal; their role was keeping revisions consistent. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. The most instructive production incidents, such as strategies silently left unmonitored, passed every gate; the operator found them by hand, and the fix each time was an interface change that makes the condition visible. We contribute the layered architecture, the two-regime account, and a production incident taxonomy, none observable in a backtest.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 3rd Workshop on LLMs and Generative AI in Finance (AI4F) at ACM ICAIF 2026 (non-archival). 11 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. | **[Tandem: Serving Hybrid Linear-Attention GUI Agents on Mobile NPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26660v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Smartphone GUI agents complete tasks in mobile apps by repeatedly reading the screen and asking a model for the next action. Agent requests retain instructions but rewrite recent history, so checkpoint policies designed for chat continuation can miss reusable prefixes. Retaining rollback states for speculative decoding can also move prompt processing off the NPU's matrix unit, adding overhead that outweighs decoding savings on short action outputs. A loaded model can further hold the NPU awake between tasks. We present Tandem, a runtime for hybrid GUI agents on mobile NPUs. Tandem saves recurrent state where consecutive prompts diverge, allowing later requests to reuse previously processed context. It keeps most prefill on the matrix unit and produces rollback states through a short per-token tail. Its verification kernel reads each weight block once for all proposed tokens. Between request bursts, Tandem releases NPU power votes without unloading the model. We implement Tandem in llama.cpp and evaluate it on recorded agent interactions across two Snapdragon generations. On a Snapdragon 8 Elite, Tandem reduces per-step latency by 30% for text-based screen descriptions and 16% for screenshots relative to llama.cpp's fastest NPU configuration, reduces energy per text step by 33%, and lowers standby power with the model loaded from 0.76 to 0.22 W.</p></details> |  |
| **[SpatialScout: Can VLM Agents Actively Explore and Map 3D Scenes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26659v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial intelligence requires agents to actively acquire visual evidence and integrate observations into a coherent representation of the three-dimensional world. We introduce SpatialScout, a benchmark for evaluating vision-language model (VLM) agents on active object-level 3D mapping. SpatialScout comprises 80 diverse indoor scenes containing 3,140 target objects. Given an object inventory, RGB observations, camera calibration, and camera poses, agents control a camera to explore each scene and estimate object centers in a shared coordinate frame. The task requires coordinating viewpoint selection, cross-view association, geometric estimation, and map revision under bounded interaction budgets. Experiments with frontier VLMs reveal substantial limitations: the strongest evaluated model, Claude Opus 5.5, achieves a mean overlap score of 41.71%, with 47.23% of targets exceeding an intersection-over-union (IoU) threshold of 0.5 and only 3.25% exceeding 0.9. These results reveal a substantial gap between recovering coarse object layouts and constructing complete, precise metric maps. SpatialScout provides a quantitative testbed for developing agents that build reliable 3D spatial representations through active perception. Code is available at https://github.com/JesseZZZZZ/SpatialScout.</p></details> |  |
| **[From TCP/IP to MCP: Security and Governance Lessons from Internet Protocols for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26624v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The past two years have seen considerable interest in protocols for managing how AI agents interact with human users, external tools, and each other. First, if AI agent protocols do not sufficiently prioritise security in the early stages of development, they risk accumulating a security deficit that becomes increasingly costly to address over time due to practical and economic challenges. Second, current governance structures for AI agent protocols centralise decision-making power in a small number of individuals and companies, raising important questions regarding legitimacy, especially if they become critical digital infrastructure. We conclude with a discussion and implications for policymakers and developers of AI agent protocols.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Do Agentic LLMs Decide to Call Tools? Behavioral ablations show that the scaffold establishes a tool-call prior; Transcoder decomposition then reveals that analysis verbs suppress this prior through features signaling that tool use is unnecessary, whereas execution verbs largely leave it intact. We therefore propose ScopeMem, a latent-memory method for scoped revision. | **[Connection Escrow: Holding AI-Agent Pod Egress in the Kernel Until an Isolated Verifier Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26501v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents in containers act by opening network connections, and the text that drives them can be hostile. Controls placed between the agent and its tools are bypassed by a child process or a direct socket, and their approval is not tied to the connection the kernel later opens. We present connection escrow: every outbound TCP connection from a supervised pod is parked inside the kernel's connect() path, before any packet exists, by a 98-instruction eBPF program on the pod's cgroup. A node interceptor attributes the held socket to its kernel socket cookie, process, executable digest, cgroup and pod identity, parks the first bytes the agent writes, and asks a verifier in a separate container with its own key. The connection is released only on a signed verdict naming that one socket cookie and destination, unexpired and unused; no verdict by the deadline resets it, and a late approval is refused. The agent is unmodified. A 36-check adversarial suite with real cgroups passes on the eBPF hold point (33 of the checks also apply, and pass, on an iptables hold point): refused connections never reach the destination; forged, rebound, replayed, expired and late verdicts are refused; the agent's attempt to reach the verifier is itself escrowed; child-process and renamed-binary bypasses fail; UDP is pinned to the resolver. On a Kubernetes cluster, unmodified pods are held per connection and attributed to their SPIFFE identity, a raw socket that bypasses a sidecar proxy is held, and 600 connections from six pods are gated. The hold adds 4.5 ms median per released connection, of which 24 us is policy. Finally, we gate on request content without terminating TLS: an eBPF uprobe reads the plaintext at the agent's TLS-library boundary (shared OpenSSL, static OpenSSL, Go crypto/tls), a model scores it, and the connection is held on that verdict while TLS stays end-to-end and the enforcer holds no key.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables. | **[VeriCUA: Learning Proactive Verification for Reliable GUI Agent Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26469v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning for computer-use agents increasingly relies on proactive reward agents that interact with the post-execution GUI environment to verify what recorded screenshots cannot reveal. Unlike prompt injection, which targets the model's instructions, information injection targets the evidence the agent trusts. (ii) For fact-poisoning templates, surface form matters more than content, since removing credibility cues such as a named source or a "BREAKING" tag drops ASR from 100% to 0% while the fake claim remains identical. | **[The Control Layer Behind Rule-Following Agents Trading Across Market Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26324v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Trading discipline often fails people. Our study tested whether four AI trading agents followed their strategies and risk rules. Most positions were simulated against live prices with a 0.09% round-trip fee; 18% involved real Hyperliquid orders. Rule adherence was high across all four agents, including the unprofitable one. Profitability tracked how often agents won relative to their break-even win rates. Institutional-style controls ran by default and contained losses through a 29% market drawdown and recovery, with documented exceptions. The discipline held for all four agents. Agents kept 97.5% of gross loss within declared stops, and typical losses closed at declared levels. Declared risk remained stable after losses and broadly stable across calm and volatile months. Twenty-three of 24 runs respected the position limit; logs identify the exception's size and duration. The same live data produced different results across assigned strategies. The trade-log tests can be rerun from the published trade snapshot; regime tests also require the published Bitcoin prices and regime labels. Section 3.5 specifies the stop-breach tolerance. These trade-level results are not account-level returns or proof of a lasting edge. | **[Mobile Embodied Agent Network (MEAN): Integrated Framework, Enabling Technologies, and Task-Driven Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26297v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mobile networks are evolving from human-centered, data-oriented communication infrastructures toward machine- and/or agent-dominant, task-oriented platforms that are to serve the rapidly increasing networked machines and embodied agents. | **[Bidirectionality is Necessary in Cellular Machines for Exact-Arrival Multi-Agent Path Finding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26272v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On ranked directed acyclic graphs coupled by vertex conflicts we study exact-arrival multi-agent path finding (departure and arrival pinned in place and time) posed as a distributed output task: every cell must emit its own bit of a valid joint path-support mask. The machines are cellular: one automaton per cell, state factored over cells, communication only along a fixed local fabric, synchronous rounds, a query-independent output round, no central decoder, and a per-topology transition code. "Necessary" here means: on an explicit hard topology, over all its feasible queries, no universally correct local-only architecture can renounce either temporal orientation; with global flags, impossibility becomes a bandwidth-round trade-off. The class is not vacuous: a flooding machine solves every instance in as many rounds as the diameter of the local fabric. The bound survives two concessions: we supply every agent's exact individual cavity, which alone solves the single-agent problem in zero rounds and makes the single-agent direction theorem vacuous; and we allow $k$ one-bit global aggregate flags. On a twelve-cell, two-agent, depth-three topology no forward-only machine with $k=0$ is correct on two feasible queries, for any number of rounds; the time-reversed topology does the same to backward-only machines. Placing $p$ copies side by side gives, for forward-only machines, $J_R+kR\ge p$ at constant depth, where $J_R$ measures how much of the query reaches the targets' cones and is $0$ here. A backward-only machine solves that family in constant rounds and a flag-streaming one in $\lceil p/k\rceil+O(1)$, so the bound is tight in order; on the composite family neither orientation can be renounced. The finite lemmas of the gadget and the pair-invariance contradictions are checked in Lean 4; the semantic bridge, the cone lemma and the counting are paper proofs. | **[CUA-Lite: An Open Platform for Computer Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26071v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open resources for computer use agents (CUAs) -- environments, datasets, and models -- remain scattered: without a standard, these resources cannot be integrated and scaled; additionally, evaluation, RL, and SFT cannot share one infrastructure. (2) Unified supervised data format, into which we convert the major public CUA datasets, from GUI grounding and understanding to full rollouts, and record our fresh rollouts from frontier CUAs on these verifiable environments. (3) Model harnesses shared across evaluation, RL, and SFT, one per model and implemented for 14 model families. Each harness runs its model in these computer environments, producing the rollouts that evaluation and RL consume, or renders stored supervised data into the model's own training format for SFT. We release reference scores across the integrated benchmarks, and show that RL and SFT through the same harness improve open-weight models. We release our dataset, code, and models, and the platform is open to contributions.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://cua-lite.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Procedural Exposure Bias in Skill-Augmented Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26062v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>External skills provide language-model agents with reusable procedural knowledge, yet how models use such knowledge after it is supplied remains poorly understood. We identify Procedural Exposure Bias (PEB), the tendency of language models to increasingly favor an action as its associated procedural pattern is repeatedly exposed in a skill, even when the interaction state and action relevance remain unchanged. We find that PEB strongly depends on how procedural knowledge is represented: presenting the same content visually substantially attenuates exposure-induced shifts in action preference relative to textual presentation. Further analysis shows that this attenuation primarily arises from visual encoding rather than structured layout. Although repeated procedural signals remain recoverable from Visual representations, they exert a weaker influence on downstream action prediction, distinguishing signal accessibility from its use as action evidence. This effect also extends to executable action selection, where increasing exposure more strongly favors the corresponding action under Text than Visual. Layer-wise analyses show that exposure-dependent action evidence becomes more pronounced toward the model output, with stronger evidence under Text, while activation interventions reveal architecture-specific roles of internal components in transmitting or counteracting the preference shift. Complete-agent evaluations across ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, and WebShop further show that Visual skills achieve higher macro-average task success than Text skills for all four evaluated models. These findings establish skill representation as an important factor governing how procedural knowledge is translated into agent decisions. All code is available at https://github.com/NEUIR/Vis-Skill.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 12 figures, 8 tables</p></details> |
| **[FinHopper: A Progressive Multi-Hop Benchmark for Complex Financial Agents with Step-wise Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26036v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Financial agents are expected to answer factual investment queries through open search, but financial multi-hop retrieval differs from general multi-hop QA in a crucial way: each intermediate answer is an exact typed constraint, such as an entity, date, event, or numerical indicator, that determines the next query. We introduce FinHopper, a benchmark for complex financial agents with controlled reasoning depth from 1 to 6 hops, covering stocks, funds, and macroeconomic facts, with gold intermediate answers for process-level evaluation. To construct FinHopper at scale, we design a state-transition-based synthesis framework that composes verified atomic financial QA patterns into dependency-preserving chains, followed by targeted in-synthesis verification, model-ensemble checking, and public-search validation by financial experts. Experiments with 20 LLM-agent configurations show that public search tools are necessary but insufficient: performance drops sharply as hop depth increases, the best model remains far below human experts, and process-aware scoring reveals partial reasoning progress hidden by final-answer-only evaluation. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/justajustin/FinHopper/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026. 28 pages, 7 figures, 8 tables. Benchmark: https://github.com/justajustin/FinHopper/</p></details> |
| **[Decision4Rec: A Jev-Inspired Framework of Decision Models on Knowledge and Active Graphs for Multi-Agent Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26035v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Decision models, such as the commercial Jev, are language models that return one probability for each option of a question instead of generating text. We present Decision4Rec, an open and modular framework that makes decision models useful for recommendation. A knowledge graph writes the logs of each recommendation scenario as short statements on a defined scale, such as "sold: top 10%". A pool of agents, proposes candidates, and a residual ranker keeps the best 30. A decision model, trained once on real choices of three scenarios, reads them through one interface of typed questions, and a fusion step combines its answers with the score of the ranker. Any model with this interface can be used, and we run the commercial Jev, the released open Kev and Kev models trained by us on recommendation requests. The framework with a trained 4B decision model is 25 to 36% above the best trained baseline in NDCG@10 in three sparse Amazon catalogues, one of them never used to train the decision model, and almost tie with baselines on the dense Steam dataset. Most of the gain comes from the pool, and with a trained recommender it comes from what the agents know about its candidates rather than from new candidates. The 27B decision model adds to the pool 5 to 6% above the 0.8B model in three of four domains. A trained 0.8B open model beats the released Jev in eleven of fifteen settings and is never worse. On MovieLens-1M, a dense domain never seen in training, the gain is 5 to 7%. Code, configurations and trained models are available at https://github.com/marcopoli/decision4rec and https://huggingface.co/m-polignano.</p></details> |  |
| **[AuDQ: Auditable Data Quality through Agentic Rule Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26031v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-stakes tabular pipelines rely on scattered, static quality checks that are hard to evolve and audit against policy intent. Raw and typed evidence both solve 32/32 authority cases, with model-dependent planning effects. Fixed-intent enforcement blocks six unsafe proposals and executes 12 eligible authorized intents. Paid integration yields 176/210 accepted benchmark-source stages, including 19/30 publication stages, recovery on 8/8 primary SWE repositories, and the most complete continuous trajectories on each of three source tasks. | **[Do Agents Need a New Kind of Paper? SPRA: Structured Paper Representation for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21424v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific agents must connect research problems, methods, and findings dispersed through narrative articles. We introduce SPRA (Structured Paper Representation for Agents), a paper-level representation of persistent research objects and typed relations. Its empirical-AI profile organizes Problems, Contributions, Components, ExperimentSetups, Measures, and Findings for querying, traversal, and reuse. Selected paper-level citation relations record dependencies, component use, and comparisons with prior work; source locations support verification. We instantiate SPRA across more than one million AI papers, with 19.95 million research units and 24.07 million internal relations. Evaluations access SPRA through Schema-RAG with task evidence fixed before inference. Across seven subfields, SPRA improves source-conditioned scientific QA by 0.381/5 over passage retrieval while remaining close to Full Compact (0.078/5 lower on average). In directed cross-paper reasoning, qualification improves by 0.184/5 over passage retrieval; Top-5 synthesis shows mixed quality effects. Across all paired RQ2 tasks, including Top-5, input+output tokens decrease by 28.5% relative to passage retrieval and 95.3% relative to Full Compact. In idea generation, a 0.067-point lower Overall score than Full Compact accompanies 93.7% fewer input+output tokens. Together, these results support SPRA as a usable representation of scientific content and a foundation for research workflows that connect, inspect, and reuse paper-level research objects. | **[Map-Distribution Mixture-of-Experts for Multi-Agent Pathfinding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25991v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing capable neural policies for multi-agent pathfinding (MAPF), which map local observations to action distributions, represents an important challenge in multi-agent learning, with practical relevance to scalable collective automation problems such as warehouse logistics. Specifically, given a collection of compact yet high-performing experts specialised for different map distributions, our architecture learns a router that selectively fuses their outputs according to each agent's local situation. We instantiate and validate this MoE approach with a commonly used graph neural network-based policy as well as a newly developed recursive action-token refinement model. Combined with supervised and reinforcement learning fine-tuning of the experts, our experiments show that MoE generally improves upon the pretrained policy and outperforms existing leading methods. At the same time, our findings reveal that appropriately trained, parameter-matched monolithic models still outperform MoE, highlighting a nuanced role for map-distribution MoE for MAPF.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLM as a Tool, Not an Agent: Code-Mined Tree Transformations for Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16555v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Neural Architecture Search (NAS) aims to automatically discover high-performing deep neural network (DNN) architectures. Our method automatically extracts reusable modules from arbitrary source code and represents full architectures as hierarchical trees, enabling evolution through reliable tree transformations rather than code generation. At each evolution step, coarse-level planning is governed by a diversity-guided algorithm that leverages Bayesian modeling to improve exploration efficiency, while the LLM resolves the remaining degrees of freedom to ensure a meaningful evolutionary trajectory and an executable generated architecture. With this formulation, instead of fully agentic LLM approaches, our method explores diverse directions beyond the inherent biases in the LLM. | **[NIOCIS: Typed-Decision Next-Hop Routing with Node-Conditioned Option Spaces for Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25985v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-use agents pay per decision: at each hop a language model re-reads the state and thinks for seconds, though its actions are finite and decisions recur. We present NIOCIS, a routing layer that folds execution logs into a loop-collapsed action graph (forward transitions plus recovery back-edges) answering one typed question: which declared option at a state, plus stop, does the fleet take next? A small model reads a calibrated distribution over them in one pass, emitting no text. On 1,615 production security-operations episodes we measure what the layer replaces, returns and is bounded by. Replaces: the typed encoder matches the logged next hop on 32.2% of the 3,875 held-out decisions (34.9% within the declared options) in 10 ms of encoder time (19.5 ms for the typed-choice form), against 2.2 s per LLM hop; two feature readouts reach 32.8% and 32.3%, an ordering the paired test does not resolve, so the typed layer is chosen for cost, not a separated lead. Tuning: jpt-4b reaches 9.9% zero-shot and 31.9% fine-tuned, a gap an exact paired test does not resolve (p=0.68), at 244 ms. Returns little: the realised hop falls outside the declared options for 7.9% of decisions and outside the model's top twenty for 9.3%; the most confident 15% holds 91.9% accuracy. Bounded: a cross-trace leave-one-out test puts the released state's ceiling at 25.6--44.9% as the state key grows more specific, i.e. repeated states disagree more often than they agree; the option-space ablation attributes the gain to per-node candidate restriction (+3.5 points over a flat catalog) rather than graph traversal (zero within its interval); a gated self-evolution loop lifts every arm above its anchor yet ends below full supervision; a confidence-gated cascade loses agreement when the escalation arm is the zero-shot stand-in; and agreement past a third of the fleet needs a state carrying the plan and the observation.</p></details> | <details><summary>34 pa...</summary><p>34 pages, 10 figures. Code and the pseudonymized derived corpus: https://github.com/geilihan/niocis</p></details> |
| **[Identifying and Mitigating Bottlenecks in Role-Playing Agents: A Systematic Study of Disentangling Character Profile Axes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.04716v5)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While Large Language Model (LLM) role-playing agents have advanced rapidly, it remains unclear which profile elements genuinely drive role-playing quality. To bridge this gap, we introduce a systematic diagnostic framework that disentangles the impact of character profiles along three axes: Familiarity (Known vs. Unknown), Structure (Structured vs. Unstructured), and Disposition (Moral vs. Immoral). Utilizing a unified hierarchical schema (5 dimensions, 28 fields), we construct a controlled dataset of 211 personas and evaluate five LLMs on both single- and multi-turn interactions. Our results reveal a striking asymmetry: \textbf{Familiarity} and \textbf{Structure} show negligible impact, while \textbf{Disposition} produces large, consistent performance degradation for immoral characters across all conditions. Further analyses suggest that the Moral--Immoral gap is amplified by post-SFT alignment, and that this degradation varies substantially across profile attributes. To mitigate this bottleneck, we propose Field-Aware Contrastive Decoding (FACD), a training-free strategy that amplifies suppressed disposition-sensitive signals, significantly closing the performance gap without sacrificing moral-character performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>EMNLP...</summary><p>EMNLP 2026 Main (Oral)</p></details> |
| **[EmbodiedWorld-200K: Toward Embodied World Agents via Embodied-Planning Dataset and Interactive World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25959v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Building embodied agents that can perceive, reason, and act across diverse 3D worlds is a central research direction toward artificial general intelligence. Yet most existing work remains confined to specific tasks in a single scene and transfers poorly to new environments, leaving general embodied agents for open-world settings under-explored. We refer to such agents as Embodied World Agents (EWAs), whose defining property is the ability to understand diverse visual environments and execute general actions in open-world conditions. Each example is equipped with multi-level annotations, ranging from low-level camera-motion trajectories to high-level semantic instructions. The dataset also provides unified evaluation splits and task-specific metrics, supporting offline pretraining and fair evaluation of EWAs. Beyond offline supervision, a defining characteristic of an EWA is its interaction with the environment; training solely on offline data is therefore insufficient, especially for generalizing to scenes not covered in the training distribution. Building dedicated real interactive environments, however, is often prohibitively expensive. To address this, we propose a novel training scheme that employs a video world model as a low-cost environment proxy and performs interactive reinforcement-learning (RL) post-training of the EWA within it. Empirically, pretraining on EmbodiedWorld-200K substantially improves the open-world embodied planning ability of the EWA, and further RL post-training inside a world model yields clear gains on out-of-distribution scenes. | **[Productive State-Space Exploration for Multi-turn RL in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25957v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Model (LLM) agents in multi-turn, long-horizon environments remains challenging due to inefficient action selection and repetitive behavioral patterns. In particular, agents often become trapped in high-confidence yet unproductive behaviors, repeatedly executing invalid actions or entering action loops that hinder task completion. We observe that these frequently violate environment preconditions, revealing a mismatch between the agent's representation of the internal state and the dynamics of the underlying environment. In addition, repetitive high-confidence actions lead to cyclical behaviors that provide little to no progress towards the goal. We argue that addressing these challenges requires more productive task-aligned exploration to improve the agent's understanding of environment dynamics. Motivated by intrinsic curiosity-driven exploration, we propose a conditional state-space exploration framework that balances task completion with intrinsic information-seeking behavior. Specifically, we introduce an intrinsic reward that penalizes actions whose observation duplicates prior state memory. This provides the benefit of reducing redundant behaviors and encouraging state-space coverage without relying on a general information-theoretic measure. Strong experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially improves policy learning in multi-turn environments, mitigating invalid actions and repetitive loops.</p></details> |  |
| **[CXBench: A Benchmark for Process-Compliant LLM Agents in Regulated Customer-Experience Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25954v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using language agents increasingly perform regulated customer-service workflows, where a correct final state is insufficient: agents must also provide disclosures, obtain consent, and follow required procedures. Existing tool-agent benchmarks largely score final business states, accepting trajectories that reach correct outcomes through non-compliant processes. We introduce cxbench, a benchmark that evaluates outcome and process compliance separately across six regulated domains. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. A hybrid grader combines deterministic checks for ordering and authority with an LLM judge for semantic requirements. We define a false-pass as an outcome-pass run that fails process checks. Across seven models and 120 tasks, 55.5% of the 840 retained executions are false-passes, representing 80.1% of outcome-passing runs; only 13.8% pass both checks. Process-inclusive evaluation changes the observed model ordering and exposes differences hidden by outcome-only scoring. cxbench makes these failures inspectable through trace-linked criteria.</p></details> |  |
| **[AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25950v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. | **[Claim Laundering: When Agent Decision Models Trust Records Over Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25947v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent decision models select actions in a single forward pass from inputs that combine environmental observations with the agent's own record of previous actions. When an action fails silently, these sources can conflict: the record reports success, but the environment shows that the step did not take effect. We ask which source determines the next decision. In all 12 configurations of four decision models and three domains, records have a larger average effect on commitment decisions than observations; after claimed-but-failed steps, the models still stop, advance, or purchase in 53-100% of cases. Yet the relevant evidence is often represented internally: linear probes on intermediate hidden states distinguish successful from failed steps on held-out apps, tasks, and goals (AUROC 0.77-1.00), and activation patching reveals increasing influence from records in later layers. We use this signal in EVIGATE, an intermediate-layer veto that requires no additional forward pass and adds 0.04-2.0% serving latency. Under injected silent failures, EVIGATE improves task success by 18.7 points in live ALFWorld agents, 5.0 in WebShop, and 5.1 in a phone simulator, and in WebShop and the simulator it also outperforms random vetoes at the same rate. Its effectiveness depends on representative calibration states: gates calibrated on offline counterfactual states do not transfer reliably to live agent trajectories.</p></details> |  |
| **[Policy Search in Code Space for Multi-Agent Pathfinding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25946v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developing coordination policies for complex multi-agent systems relies on either labor-intensive algorithm design or opaque task-specific reinforcement learning. We conducted two case studies and found that this workflow robustly outperformed any manually engineered solutions. On a challenging map in the 2024 League of Robot Runners competition, a two-day search discovered a policy that exceeds the stronger locally reproduced competition entry by 35.0% on the densest instance and generalizes to unseen map instances. In simulated real-world warehouses with irregular lanes, continuous robot geometry, and asynchronous motion, a week-long search discovered an entirely new class of multi-agent pathfinding algorithm that outperforms a calibrated proprietary solution in throughput by 58.9%. Together, the studies show that policy search in code space powered by state-of-the-art coding agents offers a highly competitive path to building real engineering solutions that are both effective and analytically tractable.</p></details> | 18 pages, 11 figures |
| **[Optimal product launch in uncertain competitive markets: A multi-agent adversarial risk analysis framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24771v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Product launching is a nuanced problem that entails choosing numerous decision variables, including timing, pricing, quality, and marketing strategy. Furthermore, decision-makers must account for multiple uncertainty sources. These include endogenous factors, such as product quality and the strategic decisions of competitors and consumers; as well as exogenous elements, like market evolution and regulations. This paper proposes a novel general framework to support product launch decisions in the presence of multiple competitors and buyers using adversarial risk analysis, mitigating the strong common-knowledge assumptions typical of standard game-theoretic approaches in this domain. We also introduce variants that account for multiple product purchases and integrate discrete choice models. To overcome the inherent computational intractability of modeling multi-agent strategic uncertainty, we deploy a simulation-optimization approach leveraging Bayesian optimization. Finally, we illustrate our proposed framework through a software launch case study focused on optimal timing, pricing, and quality decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoSkill v2: Fully Agentic Skill Evolution from Benchmark Adaptation to a Stateful Evolver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25931v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>EvoSkill v2 is a three-agent system that edits the instructions (skills) an agent is given, leaving its model and harness intact. The user starts a run by describing a benchmark and supplying a grader, which a benchmark adaptor turns into a benchmark-specific plugin. With fine-grained ground truth, EvoSkill v2's skills raised the held-out pass rate above the stronger of SkillOpt and SkillBoost by 7.7 percentage points on SpreadsheetBench v2 and by 5.4 on $τ^3$-bench banking.
